#6861. Survey Finds 84% of Organizations Reported at Least One AI-Related Incident in the Past Year

Companies are running into problems as they give artificial intelligence more control over critical business applications, with 84% reporting at least one AI-related incident in the past year, according to a new survey from identity security firm Delinea.

The company's 2026 Application Access Governance report found that some organizations have experienced incorrect financial transactions and AI actions they couldn't trace or explain. The findings raise concerns about whether enterprises have enough oversight of AI systems that can make decisions and take actions on their own

The findings are based on an independent survey of 1,000 senior finance and finance technology leaders in the United States and United Kingdom. The AI-specific analysis included 879 respondents whose organizations use the technology in critical business applications.

The report found that 35% of surveyed organizations have deployed AI agents capable of taking actions within business applications. However, only 11% of organizations using AI said their governance and security controls were keeping pace with deployment.

Delinea also found gaps in centralized visibility into agent deployment and permissions, including who owns agents and who approved their use.

Some respondents reported segregation-of-duties conflicts involving AI, while others identified agents that continued operating after the employees who deployed them had left the organization.

The research also highlighted gaps in monitoring access across business systems. Only 46% of respondents said they continuously monitor and manage cumulative access risks across applications.

Those gaps can make it difficult to identify segregation-of-duties conflicts, in which a person or agent has permissions across systems that should be kept separate.

Despite those issues, 95% of respondents said they were confident their access controls would withstand scrutiny in an external audit.

However, 26% reported access-related audit findings that had recurred in consecutive years, while 25% said some findings had remained unresolved for three or more years.

Only 22% of organizations had a single named owner for each application, with ownership tracked in one system of record.

Meanwhile, 60% reported an increase over the past two years in the number of critical applications operating outside the scope of access governance or Sarbanes-Oxley Act compliance.

The findings highlight the oversight challenges associated with giving AI agents authority to act within business systems, particularly when organizations cannot consistently identify who owns an agent or trace its actions.

Frank Vukovits, chief security scientist at Delinea, said organizations remain responsible for the actions AI agents take within their systems.

"When an agent can approve a transaction or change permissions, the business owns the outcome. You need to know what every agent can do, who owns it, and who approved it, before an auditor asks or your financials are misstated," Vukovits said.

For the full report, go to the Delinea site.

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

According to a survey by data and AI services provider Further, enterprises are reporting benefits from artificial intelligence even as their data preparation and oversight processes remain incomplete.

Today's middle school students' future careers will be transformed by artificial intelligence, automation, robotics, advanced manufacturing, biotechnology, cybersecurity, and technologies that may not yet exist. Our career education policies need to prepare them for that reality.

#6852. Report: Cyber Criminals Move Toward More Targeted Phishing Attacks

Cyber criminals are moving away from mass phishing scams and focusing on more targeted attacks, increasingly using trusted websites and services to trick victims, according to a new report from KnowBe4. The security firm found that spear-phishing attacks increased 41% over the past year.

The company's Phishing Threat Trends Report, Vol. 8 found that overall phishing volume increased 29.8% year over year. Organizations received an average of one dangerous e-mail for every 733 messages, but the bigger concern is how attackers are changing their approach.

Rather than sending the same fraudulent message to thousands of people, criminals are spending more time researching their targets and creating convincing messages that appear to come from trusted coworkers, businesses, and technology platforms.

The findings are based on threat data from KnowBe4 Defend, the company's inbound e-mail security platform, which processes approximately 1.15 billion event signals daily.

KnowBe4 found that several traditional phishing methods declined significantly compared with 2025. Mobile-focused scams dropped 47.3%, advance-fee fraud fell 31.3%, and missed-message scams declined 11.3%.

Meanwhile, spear phishing increased 41.1%, pre-attack reconnaissance rose 33.7%, and company impersonation climbed 28%.

The shift suggests that attackers are finding more value in carefully crafted scams that exploit existing business relationships and workplace routines.

Business e-mail compromise (BEC) remains particularly common, accounting for 51.2% of phishing e-mails analyzed in the report. Human resources, finance departments, and executive leadership are among the most frequently targeted groups.

Attackers also appear to be timing their campaigns around normal business activity. KnowBe4 found that 90.4% of phishing messages arrive Monday through Friday, with Monday accounting for the largest share at 20.6%.

One of the report's more troubling findings involves the growing use of legitimate technology platforms to deliver malicious content.

KnowBe4 found that phishing attacks were twice as likely to originate from legitimate infrastructure in the third quarter of 2026 compared with the previous quarter.

That includes AI services, where attackers are taking advantage of trusted domains to host fake developer consoles, login prompts, and credential-stealing pages. The report also identified a 228% increase in AI-brand impersonation attacks between March and August.

Because these malicious pages can operate on legitimate domains with valid security certificates, users may not see the usual warning signs associated with suspicious websites.

"For years, security advice has focused on a predictable checklist: Check the sender address, hover over the link, and look for the padlock," said Jack Chapman, senior vice president of threat intelligence at KnowBe4.

"While still foundational, that guidance is becoming less useful by the day."

The findings echo a recent report about management software and AI e-mail assistants. Research from Fortra and Barracuda showed how criminals are using legitimate administrative tools and hidden AI instructions to manipulate users and compromise systems.

Microsoft is combining its security information and event management and threat protection products within Microsoft Defender, creating what the company calls an Integrated Security Operations Center built for an era of AI-driven attacks.

OpenAI has introduced Dots, GPT-6 Astra-powered agents that run on their own cloud computers, using connected applications to carry on with assignments between conversations and incorporating users' feedback as they go.

#6845. This Common Drug Was Just Linked to a 24% Lower Risk of Dementia

For all of the havoc dementia causes to a person's life, scientists are still trying to figure out exactly how to prevent it. While keeping your brain and body active and following a healthy diet seems to help, there doesn't seem to be a pill you can pop to lower your risk of developing the devastating disease. But new research suggests that a common osteoporosis drug might be worth investigating further.

It's a class of medications called nitrogen-containing bisphosphonates (or NBPs). While these are typically used to strengthen bones in people with osteoporosis, NBPs could have the added benefit of supporting brain health as you age, too.

It's important to stress this now: The researchers found a link between people who used NBPs and a lower risk of dementia, but they didn't prove that the medications themselves actually dropped the risk. Still, doctors say there could be something to this. "The association is intriguing," says Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital.

Here's what experts want you to take away from the findings.

Meet the experts: Ching-Lung Cheung, PhD, study co-author and associate professor in the Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacy at the University of Hong Kong; Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center in Santa Monica, California; and Anna Hohler, MD, director of neurology for Northwell Health's Westchester Region, and chair of neurology at Phelps Hospital and Northern Westchester Hospital.

The study, which was published in the journal Alzheimer's & Dementia, analyzed the electronic medical records of more than 120,000 patients aged 60 and older in Hong Kong. All of the participants had osteoporosis or fractures due to fragility (meaning, their bones had weakened).

Researchers discovered that people who took NBPs had a 16 percent lower risk of developing Alzheimer's disease and related dementias compared to those who didn't take medication for osteoporosis. But when they compared those who used NBPs to those taking other types of osteoporosis medications, the dementia risk was 24 percent lower.

The link seemed to be especially strong in women and patients who had hip fractures.

The researchers were inspired to look into NBPs and Alzheimer’s because the drug had been shown to improve cognitive function in previous animal models of neurotoxicity and Alzheimer’s disease, says Ching-Lung Cheung, PhD, study co-author and associate professor in the Department of Pharmacology and Pharmacy at the University of Hong Kong. “These findings led us to investigate whether their use might also be associated with a lower risk of dementia in humans."

While the study didn't prove that NBPs were responsible for the lowered risk, doctors have a few theories on what could be driving this.

For one, it’s possible that the link is more indirect, according to Clifford Segil, DO, a neurologist at Providence Saint John's Health Center in Santa Monica, California. NBPs improve bone density over time, which allows people to be more active, Dr. Segil says. "Broken bones do not cause dementia but do decrease activity," he says. (Being sedentary is a risk factor for dementia.)

There may also be something about NBPs themselves that could lower the risk of dementia, though. "Some researchers speculate that these medications could influence inflammation, immune pathways, or other biological processes involved in neurodegeneration," Dr. Hoehler says. But, she points out, more research is needed.

For now, doctors don't plan to prescribe NBPs in an effort to lower dementia risk. But Dr. Cheung says it should make people feel confident about using this class of medication for bone health. "Given the low uptake of osteoporosis treatment, people with osteoporosis should consider appropriate anti-osteoporosis medication, which may offer an additional benefit for brain health," he says. Even though osteoporosis drugs are commonly available, uptake has been historically low—studies have previously estimated that around seven percent of women with osteoporosis actually take medication for it.

Ultimately, doctors say that a clinical trial is needed to see if NBPs actually lower the risk of dementia. "If future studies confirm these results, they could open an exciting new avenue for Alzheimer's prevention or treatment," Dr. Hoehler says.

Carina Hsieh, MPH, is the deputy features editor of Women’s Health. She has more than a decade’s worth of experience working in media and has covered everything from beauty, fashion, travel, lifestyle, pets, to health.

She began her career as an intern in the fashion closet at Cosmopolitan where she worked her way up to Senior Sex & Relationships Editor. While covering women’s health there, she discovered her passion for health service journalism and took a break to get her Masters in Public Health. Post-grad school, she worked as a freelance writer and as The Daily Beast’s first Beauty, Health, and Wellness Reporter.

Carina is an alum of the Fashion Institute of Technology and the Yale School of Public Health. She and her French Bulldog, Bao Bao, split their time between Brooklyn and Connecticut. She enjoys reformer Pilates, (slow) running, and smelling the fancy toiletries in boutique fitness class locker rooms.

#6844. Study: Sign-In Problems Are Primary Drivers of Help Desk Tickets

Sign-in problems account for up to 53% of help desk tickets at individual enterprises, but network issues can take significantly longer to resolve, according to a new study from ControlUp.

Analyzing 1,069,110 help desk tickets, the IT operations company identified password resets, account lockouts, and multifactor authentication (MFA) issues as major drivers of support demand.

At one large enterprise, account-related tickets took a median of 11 minutes to resolve, compared with 50 hours for network problems. Meanwhile, 87% of network and phone tickets were reassigned between teams, compared with 20% of account tickets.

MFA enrollment and re-enrollment problems appeared at 40% of organizations, with one in five linking these issues to employees replacing or upgrading their phones. Virtual desktop problems appeared at 55% of organizations analyzed.

Traditional phone support remained a major channel for IT assistance.

Among organizations that recorded how support requests were submitted, 57% reported that phone calls accounted for approximately two-thirds or more of contacts. At one organization, that figure reached 99%.

Machine-generated tickets from monitoring, security, backup, and patching systems appeared at half of the organizations studied. At 15%, automated tickets represented between 25% and 50% of all requests.

The research covered enterprises across retail, healthcare, financial services, manufacturing, construction, energy, and IT services.

ControlUp said the findings highlight the need to evaluate recurring problems and their operational impact rather than relying solely on ticket volume and resolution speed.

"IT organizations have traditionally measured support performance by how quickly they close tickets, but that doesn't tell the whole story," said Simon Townsend, senior vice president of marketing and office of the CTO at ControlUp.

"A password reset that takes a few minutes and an infrastructure failure that disrupts an entire location shouldn't carry the same operational weight."

For more information, go to the ControlUp site.

#6840. Enterprise AI Spending Outpaces Adoption Growth, Report Finds

Enterprise AI spending is growing faster than the number of organizations paying for it, according to a new Menlo Ventures report that points to a shift from buying access for employees to financing work performed by agents.

The venture capital firm's 2026 State of Enterprise AI report estimates deployment spending reached a $176.8 billion annualized run rate by September, up from $33 billion in 2025. The share of enterprises paying for AI rose from 55% to 65%.

A larger bill can reflect broader use inside an organization, but it does not independently establish that the additional work is useful or profitable.

Menlo's methodology describes revenue figures as annualized run rates. The $176.8 billion estimate therefore should not be read as $176.8 billion already spent during the first nine months of 2026.

The report also distinguishes unusually heavy spenders from the typical respondent. Average reported spending reached 4.7 times the prior year's level, while the median reached 2.2 times. That gap suggests a smaller group of large spenders drove the average substantially.

The practical implication is that an industry growth figure may be a poor benchmark for an individual organization's budget. Buyers need to understand their own workload growth and the results it produces.

"Agents are changing the economics of software," Menlo principal Derek Xiao said in the firm's announcement.

Menlo reports that agents account for 80% of tokens on OpenRouter, a service providing access to multiple models. That measures activity on one platform, rather than all enterprise AI use. Token consumption also measures processing volume, rather than completed business outcomes.

An agent that makes repeated attempts can consume more resources without delivering proportionately more value. For procurement teams, the relevant comparison is consequently the cost of completing acceptable work, including the review it requires.

Menlo estimates that Anthropic, OpenAI, and Google capture 69% of enterprise AI deployment spending. That measure is distinct from their shares of enterprise language-model API spending, a narrower category. The figures should not be used interchangeably.

The report draws on a Morning Consult survey of nearly 400 U.S. enterprise decision-makers responsible for more than $4 billion in AI spending. Market totals are modeled estimates informed by surveys, public filings, and third-party reports. Menlo discloses investments in Anthropic and OpenRouter.

A separate announcement from Information Services Group describes buyers putting more emphasis on the economics and reliability of production deployments. ISG says ongoing security and observability work remains necessary as organizations scale.

That provides a useful distinction between market demand and business value. Suppliers can generate growing revenue while customers still determine which deployments justify the cost.

For enterprise leaders, the new spending estimate strengthens the case for examining usage beyond license counts. It leaves the harder purchasing question with the customer: How much worthwhile work did the increased spending actually buy?

Menlo's State of Enterprise AI report is available here.

#6839. ‘I Picked Up Weights for the First Time in My Mid-50s. I Was Powerlifting by My 70s—Here’s How’

“Getting stronger takes patience, consistency, and a willingness to step outside my comfort zone, but the reward is so worth it.”

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

If I’ve learned anything over the past few years, it’s to just say yes. Saying the word was simple, but ended up changing my relationship with fitness—and transforming my whole life.

I’ve always loved being physically active and moving my body; I ran track and field in high school, played field hockey in both high school and college, and enjoyed horseback riding as a young adult. But shortly after graduating, I had my first of seven children at 23. From that point on, I went straight into parenting mode: My family became my priority, and with so much to juggle, exercise took a back seat for the next few decades.

But at 55, I wanted to feel strong in my body again. My kids were grown and I had more free time, so I signed up for a gym membership. I even started working with a personal trainer to make sure I was doing the right moves safely. Over the next 15 years, I exercised with several trainers, lifting light weights once or twice a week with dumbbells and weight machines. I enjoyed the workouts and saw improvements to my overall fitness, but I wasn’t particularly passionate about going to the gym, so I wasn’t always consistent and never set any real goals.

Then, at 70, everything changed. My trainer who I had come to enjoy working with and trust moved away, and without that connection, I stopped going to the gym. But after about a year away, the fitness manager at my gym, Nate Fleming, reached out to me, and I began working with him twice a week.

He immediately changed up my programming and introduced me to the idea of progressive overload. I learned that gradually increasing the weight I lifted each week could help me build muscle, get stronger, and see real results. He also added new movements to my routine, like farmer’s carries, deadlifts, and squat variations. Deadlifts, in particular, became one of my favorite exercises—I loved that the move required so much technique. There was something so satisfying about picking up a heavy weight and realizing I could lift more than I thought.

Nearly overnight, my attitude toward fitness changed: I enjoyed being challenged and discovering how much stronger I could become when I pushed myself. Over the next year, I began to see visible muscle definition in my arms and legs and felt more energized throughout the day. More importantly, though, my confidence skyrocketed because I had developed a greater trust in my own abilities.

I had grown to love lifting heavy, and wanted to step outside my comfort zone and discover what I could accomplish in my 70s. I casually asked Nate if there were deadlifting competitions for older women, and he said there were. So, I took a leap of faith and started a seven-month training process for a powerlifting competition (which took place this past March). I upped my training to three days a week, continuing to progressively increase the weights, and worked on refining my technique to move more efficiently.

The day of the competition was one of the best days of my life. I decided to compete in all three lifts: I squatted 132 pounds, bench-pressed 71.5 pounds, and deadlifted 242 pounds, setting records in all three lifts for my age and weight category in Nova Scotia, Canada, where I live. I was so proud of what I’d accomplished and felt incredibly empowered by what my body could do at 71. My kids and grandkids were also there to cheer me on, which made the day even more special. Sharing that moment with my family and showing them what was possible at my age meant so much to me. I walked away feeling stronger, more confident, and excited to do it again.

My next powerlifting competition is this month, and I still lift with Nate three days a week. My training is structured in four-week blocks, and the weight, sets, and reps change each week. For instance, in week one, I might do 3 sets of 5 reps at a moderate weight. Then, I gradually increase the load in weeks two and three while lowering the reps as I get closer to my heaviest lifts. Week four is a deload week, so I reduce the weight and give my body a chance to recover before lifting heavier again.

Each workout starts with the main lift (deadlift, squat, or bench press) because that’s when I have the most energy. Then, I move on to three or four accessory exercises, where I use lighter weights to strengthen supporting muscles, address weaknesses, and improve my performance in the main lifts. For example, on deadlift day, I might incorporate exercises like lunges, hamstring curls, and bent-over rows to strengthen my glutes, hamstrings, and back.

I’m incredibly proud of my progress over the last six months. I have a lingering shoulder injury, so right now, my bench press has stayed about the same at 71.5 pounds. But I can squat 160 pounds and deadlift 265 pounds, which is only five pounds away from the Canadian deadlift record.

While I train hard, I recover even harder in between sessions. I get seven to eight hours of sleep each night and am mindful about nutrition, eating lots of protein, since it’s important for building and maintaining muscle. I aim for around 120 grams a day, with eggs, chicken, and fish as some of my go-to sources. Additionally, during rest days, I focus on stretching and hip and shoulder mobility to improve my range of motion. I don’t count my steps, but I try to walk as much as I can throughout the day to keep my body moving and maintain cardiovascular health.

I love Feetures socks for their snug arch support and gentle compression that keeps them comfortably in place during workouts.

Clif Builders are my favorite post-workout snack since they taste great and pack 20 g of protein per bar.

When I first decided to work with a personal trainer in my 50s, I wanted guidance, accountability, and the confidence that I was exercising safely and effectively—and I got that. My first few trainers helped me build a foundation by teaching me proper form and the fundamental movements that I still rely on today.

But working with Nate completely changed my relationship with lifting: He knows how to motivate me, pushing me without going beyond my abilities. When I attempt a new personal record, his belief in me helps me believe in myself, too. Now, I know what I’m capable of and how to challenge myself while lifting safely. The gym has become so much more than a place to work out—it’s where I keep discovering what I can do.

I’ve learned that getting stronger isn’t just about how hard I work in the gym—it’s also about how well I recover between workouts through getting plenty of sleep, mobility work, stretching, and walking. When I don’t make time for recovery activities or I don’t fuel properly, it makes a difference; I feel more fatigued, have less energy during workouts, and struggle to perform at the same level.

For me, progress doesn’t mean pushing myself to the limit every day. It means finding the right balance between challenging my body and giving it the rest and support it needs to keep getting stronger.

If you’d told me a few years ago that I’d be powerlifting in my 70s and chasing a deadlift record, I never would have believed you. Before I started training with Nate, I had never really set big fitness goals or thought about pushing my limits. But that began to change when I realized how much stronger I could become with the right coaching and encouragement. I was inspired to set goals that challenged me in new ways I never thought possible, and I’ve learned that it’s never too late to surprise myself and try something new. Having a goal to work toward keeps me motivated, and I love seeing how far I can push myself when I have the right support and plan in place.

Of course, getting stronger takes patience, consistency, and a willingness to step outside my comfort zone, but the reward is so worth it. I hope my journey encourages other women, especially those in their 60s and 70s, to keep challenging themselves and try new things. They might discover a passion they never knew they had, just as I did.

Andi Breitowich is a freelance writer who covers health, fitness, relationships, beauty, and smart living. She is a graduate of Emory University and Northwestern University's Medill School of Journalism. Her work has appeared in Women's Health, POPSUGAR, Food & Wine, What To Expect, Cosmopolitan, Men's Health, and elsewhere. As a former collegiate pole vaulter, she loves all things fitness and has yet to meet a group workout class she doesn't like.

#6837. Phishing Threats Totaled 7.3 Billion in Q3 2026

E-mail phishing threats reached 7.3 billion in Q3 2026, with September activity increasing 41% from August, according to Microsoft Threat Intelligence.

According to a report from Microsoft Threat Intelligence and the Microsoft Defender Security Research Team, phishing detections reached nearly 3 billion in September alone.

The findings also highlighted changes in attacker techniques, including business-service impersonation, credential theft, and social engineering through Microsoft Teams.

The Redmond-based firm recognized approximately 129 million malicious messages spoofing the sender domains of tracked business services during Q3.

DocuSign was the most frequently spoofed brand in September, accounting for 29% of the month's tracked spoofing activity. Adobe, SurveyMonkey, and Barracuda also experienced increases toward the end of the quarter.

The research found that some malicious messages were delivered despite failing e-mail authentication checks and being identified as threats.

In September, tenant- or user-level security overrides applied to 63% of these messages with a recorded security outcome — most involved tenant-level exceptions.

Microsoft warned that overly broad exceptions intended to support legitimate business communications can weaken protection against impersonation attacks.

The company emphasized that these findings do not establish whether recipients opened the messages or whether accounts were compromised.

Microsoft also recorded approximately 141,000 malicious Teams call attempts during Q3.

Attackers increasingly used ordinary personal names instead of explicitly impersonating IT support personnel. Such display names accounted for 46% of an observed sample in July, rising to 88% in September.

Meanwhile, QR code phishing declined 67% compared to Q2, totaling roughly 12.1 million attacks.

CAPTCHA-gated phishing activity increased toward the end of the quarter, reaching 3.1 million detected attacks in September. SVG attachments played a significant role in the increase.

Credential theft remained the primary objective of malicious attachment campaigns, with approximately 94% to 96% of payload-based attacks directing users to phishing sites or fraudulent sign-in pages.

Microsoft recommended reviewing Exchange Online Protection and Defender for Office 365 configurations, enabling Safe Links and Safe Attachments, and strengthening authentication protections.

The company also advised organizations to review security exceptions and verify the identities of external Teams contacts before responding to suspicious requests.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

#6835. Mel Robbins Started Lifting Weights in Her 50s. Here’s What Her Gym Routine Looks Like

The podcast host breaks it down on the latest episode of Strong Like.

Women's Health may earn commission from the links on this page, but we only feature products we believe in. Why Trust Us?

Mel Robbins has always been a mover—running, hiking, skiing, Pilates, yoga, you name it—but it’s only been recently that the 58-year-old has added strength training to her fitness regimen. She credits Dr. Stacy Sims (a Women’s Health advisory board member) and Dr. Vonda Wright, both guests on The Mel Robbins Podcast, with finally persuading her to lift weights.

“They kept saying, when we reach a certain age, 40 and up, for us to start resistance training and with heavier weights,” says Mel, who had to overcome a few fitness myths to finally prioritize pumping iron. “I was nervous I would bulk up. That's a joke, because you know how much you need to train to actually bulk up? I wasn't going to be doing that.”

So what exactly does Mel do in the gym? She walked WH through a typical session for the latest episode of Strong Like.

Before Mel’s feet ever touch a mat, she makes sure to jot down—with actual pen and paper!—what she intends to do at the gym. “This will ensure that you actually do it, because if you're relying on this noggin up here, it ain't happening,” she says.

With her to-do list by her side, Mel begins her session with a warm-up. She runs through a batter of stretches that include inchworms (with an added push-up—go Mel!) and lunges with a twist, before moving on to a banded overhead press to mobilize her shoulders. “I've stopped judging these little bands,” Mel says. “These little suckers look like nothing. Let me tell you, there's a whole lot of something in these bands. You'll be shocked at how much gain you can get and how great this is for you.”

With her warm-up complete, she moves on to the workout, shifting the bands from her arms to her legs for a duck walk. Mimicking the water fowl, she moves forward and back, side to side, keeping outward pressure on the bands.

Next, she tackles one of her favorite moves, a reverse lunge with medicine ball twist. “It's a compound movement that works mobility, your core, your arms, your legs, everything in one movement,” Mel says. “It's super-simple, painful, and awful, and it works like a charm.”

She moves on to the upper body with chest presses, though she has a hack for lighting up even more muscles in the process. “If you want to maximize what you're doing here, suck in your stomach and clench your butt, because then it's not just arms; it's a whole-body thing,” she says.

Finally, she concludes with a snatch, joking: “You can work out a lot of aggression at the world doing [these].”

Ultimately, says Mel, her approach to fitness is pretty straightforward: “I'm not in here because I want to look good in a bathing suit. I do, but that's not the motivation. I'm in here lifting weights because I want to live a long, vibrant, healthy life. I want to be dancing into my late 90s. I want to make sure I can pick up the suitcase and put it up in the overhead. I want to make sure that I am strong so my life is long.”

To hear more about Mel’s gym motivation, click play on the latest episode of Strong Like above!

Beyond vacuums and mops, the Purifier Hot+Cool is $210 off—its lowest price yet. It combines an air purifier, space heater, and fan, making it a practical year-round addition to a room. The bladeless design includes a remote that attaches magnetically to the top, and an LCD screen that indicates when it’s time to replace the filter.

The editor-backed V8 Cyclone is currently $150 off and comes with attachments designed to help clean hard-to-reach spots, including in your car. Want an all-in-one wet-dry vacuum?
